=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a grid of H \times W cells. Let (i, j) denote the cell at the i-th row from the top and the j-th column from the left.
Cell (i, j) is empty if S_{i,j} is ., and blocked if it is #.
Count the number of ways to start from an empty cell and make K moves to adjacent cells (up, down, left, or right), without passing through blocked squares and not visiting the same cell more than once.
Specifically, count the number of sequences of length K+1, ((i_0, j_0), (i_1, j_1), \dots, (i_K, j_K)), satisfying the following.

- 1 \leq i_k \leq H, 1 \leq j_k \leq W, and S_{i_k, j_k} is ., for each 0 \leq k \leq K.
- |i_{k+1} - i_k| + |j_{k+1} - j_k| = 1 for each 0 \leq k \leq K-1.
- (i_k, j_k) \neq (i_l, j_l) for each 0 \leq k < l \leq K.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W K
S_{1,1}S_{1,2}\dots S_{1,W}
S_{2,1}S_{2,2}\dots S_{2,W}
\vdots
S_{H,1}S_{H,2}\dots S_{H,W}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq H, W \leq 10
- 1 \leq K \leq 11
- H, W, and K are integers.
- Each S_{i,j} is . or #.
- There is at least one empty cell.

Sample Input 1

2 2 2
.#
..

Sample Output 1

2

Here are the two possible paths:

- (1,1) \rightarrow (2,1) \rightarrow (2,2)
- (2,2) \rightarrow (2,1) \rightarrow (1,1)

Sample Input 2

2 3 1
.#.
#.#

Sample Output 2

0

Sample Input 3

10 10 11
....#..#..
.#.....##.
..#...##..
...#......
......##..
..#......#
#........#
..##......
.###....#.
...#.....#

Sample Output 3

218070

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1000000)
def main():
    input=sys.stdin.readline
    H,W,K=map(int,input().split())
    grid=[input().strip() for _ in range(H)]
    N=H*W
    # map each cell index to list neighbor indices that are empty
    # We can compute on fly with bit operations? Precompute neighbor masks maybe.
    empty=[False]*N
    for i in range(H):
        for j in range(W):
            if grid[i][j]=='.': empty[i*W+j]=True
    # precompute neighbor lists for each empty cell
    neighbors=[[] for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(H):
        for j in range(W):
            idx=i*W+j
            if not empty[idx]: continue
            for di,dj in dirs:
                ni,nj=i+di,j+dj
                if 0<=ni<H and 0<=nj<W and empty[ni*W+nj]:
                    neighbors[idx].append(ni*W+nj)
    # Maybe sort? no.
    ans=0
    # Use recursive function inside main
    def dfs(pos, depth, mask):
        if depth == K:
            return 1
        total=0
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit=1<<nb
            if not (mask & bit):
                total += dfs(nb, depth+1, mask | bit)
        return total
    for start in range(N):
        if empty[start]:
            ans += dfs(start, 0, 1<<start)
    print(ans)

Option 1: Use recursive DFS for first half:
first_masks = [[] for _ in range(N)]
def dfs_first(pos, depth, mask):
    if depth == a:
        first_masks[pos].append(mask)
        return
    for nb in neighbors[pos]:
        bit=1<<nb
        if not mask & bit:
            dfs_first(nb, depth+1, mask|bit)
for start in empty_cells:
    dfs_first(start, 0, 1<<start)
This enumerates all first half paths. Number of calls sum prefixes <= a. For a=5, small.

Then second half:

    nonlocal ans? In Python nested function with nonlocal. At depth == b:
        # count first masks for this pos (middle m) that intersect only at pos
        bitpos = 1<<pos
        cnt=0

        ans += cnt

        bit=1<<nb
        if not mask & bit:

for m in empty_cells:

Let's formalize MITM:

Let H,W,K. Build graph of empty cells. Let N=H*W. For each cell index v, neighbors[v] list of adjacent empty cell indices.

Choose a = K // 2, b = K - a. This ensures a <= b and b <= a+1. For K=11, a=5,b=6. For K=10, a=5,b=5. For K=1, a=0,b=1. For K=2, a=1,b=1. For K=3, a=1,b=2. For K=4, a=2,b=2. For K=5, a=2,b=3. For K=6, a=3,b=3. For K=7, a=3,b=4. For K=8, a=4,b=4. For K=9, a=4,b=5.

Enumerate all simple paths of exactly a edges (a+1 vertices) starting from any empty cell. For each such path ending at v, store its visited mask in first_masks[v]. Note: If a=0, path is just start v, mask=1<<v.

Then enumerate all simple paths of exactly b edges starting from any empty cell v (the middle). For each such path, let mask2 be visited mask. Count how many masks in first_masks[v] satisfy (mask1 & mask2) == (1 << v). Add to answer.

Why does this count each full path exactly once? Given a valid full path of length K, let v = vertex at position a. The prefix of length a is in first_masks[v], the suffix of length b starting at v is enumerated. They intersect only at v, so counted. Conversely, any counted pair concatenates to a valid path. The pair is unique because v is fixed at position a and prefix/suffix unique. Good.

Potential issue: The first half path and second half path are both directed. The prefix is directed from start to v; the suffix is directed from v to end. Concatenation direction is start -> v -> end. This matches sequence. Good.

Need be careful operator precedence: `fm & mask == bitpos` in Python is `(fm & mask) == bitpos`? Bitwise & has higher precedence than ==? In Python, bitwise AND has higher precedence than comparisons, so yes `fm & mask == bitpos` is `(fm & mask) == bitpos`. But for clarity use parentheses.

Need make `ans` nonlocal in nested function. In Python, if we define `ans = 0` in main and `def dfs_second(...): nonlocal ans`, okay. But if final code at top-level, nonlocal not allowed unless inside main. We'll wrap in main.

Alternatively, avoid nonlocal by having dfs_second return count:
def dfs_second(pos, depth, mask):
    if depth == b:
        bitpos = 1 << pos
        cnt = 0
        for fm in first_masks[pos]:
            if (fm & mask) == bitpos:
                cnt += 1
        return cnt
    total = 0
    for nb in neighbors[pos]:
        bit = 1 << nb
        if not (mask & bit):
            total += dfs_second(nb, depth+1, mask | bit)
    return total
ans = sum(dfs_second(v,0,1<<v) for v in empty_cells)
This avoids nonlocal and is clean. Function calls for second enumeration ~ prefixes length <=6, 121k, fine. Return int. Use recursion. Good.

Could use bitmasks for neighbors to speed enumeration:
neighbor_masks = [0]*N
for v in empty_cells:
    for nb in neighbors[v]:
        neighbor_masks[v] |= 1<<nb
Then in DFS:
avail = neighbor_masks[pos] & ~mask
while avail:
    lsb = avail & -avail
    nb = lsb.bit_length() - 1

Let's write code:

import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 20)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    H, W, K = map(int, input().split())
    grid = [input().strip() for _ in range(H)]
    N = H * W
    bits = [1 << i for i in range(N)]
    empty = []
    is_empty = [False] * N
    for i in range(H):
        row = grid[i]
        for j, ch in enumerate(row):
            if ch == '.':
                idx = i * W + j
                is_empty[idx] = True
                empty.append(idx)

    neighbors = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    dirs = [(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)]
    for idx in empty:
        i, j = divmod(idx, W)
        for di, dj in dirs:
            ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < H and 0 <= nj < W:
                nidx = ni * W + nj
                if is_empty[nidx]:
                    neighbors[idx].append(nidx)

    a = K // 2
    b = K - a

    first_masks = [[] for _ in range(N)]

    def dfs_first(pos, depth, mask):
        if depth == a:
            first_masks[pos].append(mask)
            return
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if not (mask & bit):
                dfs_first(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)

    for s in empty:
        dfs_first(s, 0, bits[s])

    def dfs_second(pos, depth, mask):
        if depth == b:
            bitpos = bits[pos]
            cnt = 0
            for fm in first_masks[pos]:
                if (fm & mask) == bitpos:
                    cnt += 1
            return cnt
        total = 0
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if not (mask & bit):
                total += dfs_second(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)
        return total

    ans = 0
    for s in empty:
        ans += dfs_second(s, 0, bits[s])
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think of if there is any issue with Python recursion and closures causing `first_masks` lookup in dfs_second leaf. `first_masks` is a free variable, cell access. Could bind as default argument to speed:
def dfs_second(pos, depth, mask, first_masks=first_masks, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits, b=b):
    ...
But recursive call `dfs_second(nb, depth+1, mask | bit)` will use default. This makes `first_masks`, `neighbors`, `bits`, `b` local variables (defaults) instead of free variables, faster. Similarly dfs_first. Let's implement with default args for performance.

Code:
    def dfs_first(pos, depth, mask, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits, first_masks=first_masks, a=a):
        if depth == a:
            first_masks[pos].append(mask ^ bits[pos])
            return
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if not (mask & bit):
                dfs_first(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)

    def dfs_second(pos, depth, mask, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits, first_masks=first_masks, b=b):
        if depth == b:
            other = mask ^ bits[pos]
            cnt = 0
            for fm in first_masks[pos]:
                if (fm & other) == 0:
                    cnt += 1
            return cnt
        total = 0
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if not (mask & bit):
                total += dfs_second(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)
        return total

Default args are evaluated at definition, okay. `a` and `b` are local variables in main; default captures. This avoids cell variable access. Good.

Could also bind `first_masks_pos = first_masks[pos]` in leaf.

        for nb in neighbors[pos]:

            if mask & bit:
                continue
            total += dfs_second(...)
This might be slightly faster. Let's use `if mask & bit: continue` for clarity? Both fine. Use `if not (mask & bit):` maybe okay. I'll use `if mask & bit: continue` to reduce indentation? Let's write:

        for nb in neighbors[pos]:

            total += dfs_second(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)

Let's maybe prove MITM in my mind one more time:

Define set P_a(v) = {mask of vertices excluding v of a simple path of length a ending at v}. For each full valid path p=(p0,...,pK), let v=p_a. The prefix p0..pa is in P_a(v) with mask A = {p0,...,p_{a-1}} (excluding v). The suffix pa..pK is a simple path of length b starting at v; when DFS reaches it, mask B = {p_{a+1},...,p_K} (excluding v). Since p is simple, A ∩ B = ∅, so counted. Conversely, if A ∈ P_a(v), B is suffix mask from v, and A∩B=∅, then concatenating the prefix sequence (which ends at v) with suffix sequence (which starts at v) gives a valid sequence of length K with no repeats. The prefix sequence is uniquely associated with A? Wait, P_a(v) stores masks only, not the sequence. If two different prefix sequences have the same mask A, they are stored as duplicate entries. When counting, we iterate over entries, so each prefix sequence is counted. The suffix sequence is generated by DFS; each suffix sequence corresponds to one leaf. Thus pair of entries corresponds to a pair of sequences. Good.

Let's maybe write final code now in mind:

```python
import sys

sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 20)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    H, W, K = map(int, input().split())
    grid = [input().strip() for _ in range(H)]

    N = H * W
    bits = [1 << i for i in range(N)]

    is_empty = [False] * N
    empty = []
    for i in range(H):
        for j, ch in enumerate(grid[i]):
            if ch == '.':
                idx = i * W + j
                is_empty[idx] = True
                empty.append(idx)

    neighbors = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    dirs = ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1))
    for idx in empty:
        i, j = divmod(idx, W)
        for di, dj in dirs:
            ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < H and 0 <= nj < W:
                nidx = ni * W + nj
                if is_empty[nidx]:
                    neighbors[idx].append(nidx)

    a = K // 2
    b = K - a

    first_masks = [[] for _ in range(N)]

    def dfs_first(pos, depth, mask, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits, first_masks=first_masks, a=a):
        if depth == a:
            first_masks[pos].append(mask ^ bits[pos])
            return
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if mask & bit:
                continue
            dfs_first(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)

    for s in empty:
        dfs_first(s, 0, bits[s])

    def dfs_second(pos, depth, mask, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits, first_masks=first_masks, b=b):
        if depth == b:
            fms = first_masks[pos]
            if not fms:
                return 0
            other = mask ^ bits[pos]
            cnt = 0
            for fm in fms:
                if (fm & other) == 0:
                    cnt += 1
            return cnt
        total = 0
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if mask & bit:
                continue
            total += dfs_second(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)
        return total

    ans = 0
    for s in empty:
        ans += dfs_second(s, 0, bits[s])

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's see if in grid, a set of 6 vertices could have two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint but different start. Suppose set forms a cycle with a chord? For a simple path on a set, the start is the other endpoint of the Hamiltonian path. If there are two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint, the starts could differ. Is that possible in a grid graph? Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

Now, let's think of if there is any issue with "first_masks" storing masks without middle but not storing the actual sequence; if two paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are separate entries, so counted. Good.

If two different suffix sequences from same middle have same mask and same endpoint, they are generated separately by DFS and counted separately. Good.

Actually mask without pos includes start and intermediate; if same mask and same end, start is one of the vertices in mask. Could there be two different starts within same mask? The mask is a set of 5 vertices; a simple path of length 5 ending at pos has 5 other vertices. The start is one of them. If two paths have same set and same end, they might have different starts if the set has multiple Hamiltonian paths ending at pos. Example possible? Let's see if in grid, a set of 6 vertices could have two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint but different start. Suppose set forms a cycle with a chord? For a simple path on a set, the start is the other endpoint of the Hamiltonian path. If there are two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint, the starts could differ. Is that possible in a grid graph? Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. For a simple path on a set, the start is the other endpoint of the Hamiltonian path. If there are two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint, the starts could differ. Is that possible in a grid graph? Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. Let's see if in grid, a set of 6 vertices could have two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint but different start. Suppose set forms a cycle with a chord? For a simple path on a set, the start is the other endpoint of the Hamiltonian path. If there are two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint, the starts could differ. Is that possible in a grid graph? Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

For a simple path on a set, the start is the other endpoint of the Hamiltonian path. If there are two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint, the starts could differ. Is that possible in a grid graph? Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

For a simple path on a set, the start is the other endpoint of the Hamiltonian path. If there are two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint, the starts could differ. Is that possible in a grid graph? Let's try to construct: Set vertices {0,1,2,3} with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2 (a triangle with tail). Hamiltonian paths ending at 3: 0-1-2-3 and 1-0-2-3? 1-0-2-3 uses edges 1-0,0-2,2-3, valid. Same set {0,1,2,3}, same end 3, starts 0 and 1. Is this graph a grid graph? Vertices 0,1,2,3 with edges 0-1,1-2,2-3,0-2. This is a path 0-1-2-3 plus chord 0-2. In a grid, can we have a 4-cycle? 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,0)? Edges 0-1,0-2,1-2? 1 and2 diagonal not adjacent. Need chord 0-2 adjacent, so 0 and2 must be adjacent. Let's set 0=(0,0),1=(0,1),2=(1,1),3=(1,2)? Edges 0-1,1-2,2-3, and 0-2? 0=(0,0),2=(1,1) diagonal not adjacent. To have 0-2 adjacent and 1-2 adjacent and 0-1 adjacent, 0,1,2 form a triangle, impossible in grid (no triangles). Grid graph is bipartite and has no K3. Could there be two Hamiltonian paths with same endpoint in a grid graph? Possibly with larger sets. But even if yes, our list stores duplicates if DFS reaches them separately. If two different paths have same mask and same endpoint, they are distinct sequences and should both be counted. Our DFS will append both if both exist. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 20)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    H, W, K = map(int, input().split())
    grid = [input().strip() for _ in range(H)]

    N = H * W
    bits = [1 << i for i in range(N)]

    is_empty = [False] * N
    empty = []
    for i in range(H):
        for j, ch in enumerate(grid[i]):
            if ch == '.':
                idx = i * W + j
                is_empty[idx] = True
                empty.append(idx)

    neighbors = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    dirs = ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1))

    for idx in empty:
        i, j = divmod(idx, W)
        for di, dj in dirs:
            ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < H and 0 <= nj < W:
                nidx = ni * W + nj
                if is_empty[nidx]:
                    neighbors[idx].append(nidx)

    a = K // 2
    b = K - a

    # first_masks[v] stores masks of the first half paths ending at v,
    # with the vertex v removed from the mask.
    first_masks = [[] for _ in range(N)]

    def dfs_first(pos, depth, mask, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits,
                  first_masks=first_masks, a=a):
        if depth == a:
            first_masks[pos].append(mask ^ bits[pos])
            return
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if mask & bit:
                continue
            dfs_first(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)

    for s in empty:
        dfs_first(s, 0, bits[s])

    def dfs_second(pos, depth, mask, neighbors=neighbors, bits=bits,
                   first_masks=first_masks, b=b):
        if depth == b:
            fms = first_masks[pos]
            if not fms:
                return 0
            other = mask ^ bits[pos]
            cnt = 0
            for fm in fms:
                if (fm & other) == 0:
                    cnt += 1
            return cnt

        total = 0
        for nb in neighbors[pos]:
            bit = bits[nb]
            if mask & bit:
                continue
            total += dfs_second(nb, depth + 1, mask | bit)
        return total

    ans = 0
    for s in empty:
        ans += dfs_second(s, 0, bits[s])

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
